### [Help your friends get ADHD and depression meds](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8qYuy9Cr6BNpgjJjT/help-your-friends-get-adhd-and-depression-meds)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For all my efforts at deliberate practicing rationality, by far the most actionable way to self-improve is "get on ADHD or depression meds, if you need them."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your friends with ADHD and depression struggle to summon the motivation and focus necessary to get themselves a teledoc who can give them such meds. One of the most useful things you can do is sit them down and say "hey, wanna do the steps here to get some meds?" and then stare at them until they do. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(or, until they say "really I do not want that actually.")</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This brought to you by ~2 years of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pZrvkZzL2JnbRgEBC/feedbackloop-first-rationality"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Feedbackloop-first Rationality</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ajT6q5aMokagfthvG/hire-or-become-a-thinking-assistant"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking Assistance</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8qYuy9Cr6BNpgjJjT/help-your-friends-get-adhd-and-depression-meds#comments">Discuss</a>

### [METR: Independent Assessment of AI R&D Acceleration at Anthropic in October 2026](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/N5AmYkRks5CqAxvBg/metr-independent-assessment-of-ai-r-and-d-acceleration-at)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <blockquote><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Executive summary</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From August 12 to October 29, 2026, METR staff performed a high-access evaluation at Anthropic to assess the degree to which AI is accelerating AI R&amp;D. If AI systems started to significantly accelerate further AI development, this could lead to a </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36054"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sudden emergence of extreme and risky capabilities</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic offered unprecedented access to internal data on their AI R&amp;D process, including a variety of metrics on pretraining, reinforcement learning (RL), training compute in each domain, benchmark scores for internal models, AI speedup analyses, forecasts of a future model’s metrics, and interviews with relevant staff.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In our first section, </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#our-conclusions-on-risk"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we share our subjective conclusions about risk</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We now believe that:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As of October 2026, Anthropic is likely improving the capabilities of their models on well-specified tasks at around twice the rate they did between March 2024 and October 2025. This estimate is given with high uncertainty; we would not be surprised if the true acceleration was closer to 1.3x or 3x the historical rate.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A majority of this acceleration today is likely attributable to Anthropic’s extensive use of AI to do R&amp;D, though there is significant uncertainty and other causes likely played a large role in acceleration in late 2025.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The lack of observed bottlenecks or strong diminishing returns means there is a significant risk that further capabilities progress could lead to even faster acceleration, though, as we say below, we may be missing important bottlenecks.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In our second section we share some of the </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#key-findings"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">key empirical findings from our Full Report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since October 2025, benchmark scores (measured via AECI, Anthropic’s version of the Epoch Capabilities Index) for Anthropic’s models have been growing at an estimated 1.9x the pre-October 2025 rate [95% confidence interval: 1.5–2.8x], implying that Anthropic has made roughly two years of historical progress over the last year.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are confident that the acceleration was not due to an acceleration in the scaling of training compute.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Interviews and surveys are consistent with AI usage causing a doubling in the pace of capabilities progress. Anthropic staff self-report dramatic uplift from use of AI systems in interviews and surveys, although these are challenging to interpret. We were told that R&amp;D is accelerated primarily via AI assistance rather than fully autonomous contributions, but there are some signs that fully autonomous contributions will play a larger role soon.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We did not see evidence for significant bottlenecks which would prevent further capabilities progress due to limits on algorithmic progress, training compute, experiment compute, or data in the next 12 months. However, our understanding of the status of different bottlenecks changed several times as we received more detailed information, so we are not confident in our current assessment.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some of the key findings that most informed our conclusions about risk were redacted by Anthropic.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In our third section, we describe the </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#process-details"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">assessment process</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The scope, setup, and timeline of this assessment.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Details of the information shared with METR.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Details of what information and evidence we were not able to share given Anthropic’s redactions.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finally, we describe the biggest </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#limitations"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">limitations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of our analysis:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We could be underestimating capability acceleration if we did not assign enough importance to (1) evidence that some growth rates have further increased recently; (2) further automation of AI R&amp;D tasks leading to a further acceleration in progress; (3) evidence that AECI growth has sometimes understated growth in real-world impact.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We could be overestimating capability acceleration if we did not assign enough importance to (1) non-AI factors in Anthropic’s research progress; (2) AECI’s focus on verifiable tasks; (3) reasons why Anthropic’s early growth rate (2023–2025) was unusually low.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The remainder of the report goes into detailed discussion of each of the key components. We include </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/#appendices"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">appendices</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with supplemental material.</span></p></blockquote><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793907265/lexical_client_uploads/qzt7uuyt3ygicyt7xxwx.png" /><p><a href="https://x.com/METR_Evals/status/2118417772774580623" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Twitter thread</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/RyanGreenblatt/status/2118447235289817503" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ryan Greenblatt</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This report on AI R&amp;D automation (RSI) is a big update towards ASI within 1-2 years. It says AI progress is sped up by RSI right now by around 2x. In AI 2027, this happens around March 2027.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was seriously wrong about RSI speed up; I previously expected much less in 2026 (around 1.15x-1.3x). It seems as though there was specific privately available evidence that would have changed my mind.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I haven't seen an unredacted version of this report; this is purely about the public report. Because so much of the underlying evidence was redacted from the public report, I think conclusions about things other than the observed 2x faster rate of progress on benchmarks depend significantly on trust for the authors of the report. (E.g., conclusions about whether this speed up is from RSI depend on trust.) I do trust the authors of the report a lot to be careful and rigorous and generally do a good job. (Note that I also work at METR.) Less extensive redactions would greatly reduce this need for trust.</span></p></blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ajeya_cotra/status/2118448038356390177" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ajeya Cotra</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our colleagues found that Anthropic’s capabilities progress has doubled, mainly due to AI. Ryan’s modeling from public info suggests the whole company’s R&amp;D would be going 4-5x faster. Labor uplift would be higher still. This is a major update to very short timelines.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am sad that so much of the underlying evidence in this report was redacted. I think evidence transparency is very important for the world to figure this out in time. </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/evidence-about-risk-should-be-transparent" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence about risk should be transparent</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/DKokotajlo/status/2118475061934055533" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Daniel Kokotajlo</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: says the METR report confirms his worry from two days ago, which was:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We at AIFP are spooked by [Anthropic's S1 update]. IF (big IF) Anthropic really is imminently about to meet the RSP threshold of 2x acceleration over baseline, then it seems timelines to full RSI and superintelligence may be very short indeed. For example if we naively optimize the AIFM parameters to achieve 2x acceleration over baseline progress in Jan 2027, we get superintelligence sooner than depicted in AI 2027.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See also </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YuKktzP9yGbdrd7ry/gradientdissenter-s-shortform?commentId=DyGgEva4zjmakprdB" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GradientDissenter's take</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/N5AmYkRks5CqAxvBg/metr-independent-assessment-of-ai-r-and-d-acceleration-at#comments">Discuss</a>

### [People Should Resolve Tensions In AI Safety Research](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tn4WSyrqr7ozGLjRy/people-should-resolve-tensions-in-ai-safety-research)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By now, there are lots of AI safety research papers, more than what is reasonable for a single person to read. This will get worse as the machines get better at AI safety research, and as more talent and resources pour into the field. But the "paper" is an arbitrary unit, we really care about if we make progress on an "uncertainty" or an "issue", which papers provide heterogeneous and sometimes contradictory evidence towards. Right now, I loosely claim that we should have more short writeups that simply compile evidence from papers (particularly contradictory evidence), and reconcile them into an answer for a question. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why?</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Object level, it's good for the field.</span></b><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's hard to sift through all these papers! Organising them is useful and saves time.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Orienting towards research questions is a good way to organise our understanding so that we can productively identify gaps and write papers. This was partly what motivated </span><a href="https://midtraining.arcadiaimpact.org" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Stress-Testing Alignment Midtraining</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Interesting progress comes out of contradictions, where people have differing intuitions and experimental results. Methodological errors can also be exposed if we have two things that should be the same, but are actually different.</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's good for field-building reasons.</span></b><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's probably better than the average experiment cooked up by a person + Claude. And, it's cheaper to fail here.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most people probably read too few papers, so this could also improve the average experiment done by someone, as a forced related work.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The writeups themselves are more likely to be accessible to newcomers when it comes to understanding important questions in the field. </span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's worth it for people at a variety of seniority levels to do this, not just very new people. But for more senior people, this tends to look something like laying out a research agenda anyway.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's a (rushed, not great) example:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Does reward hacking learned from RL also cause emergent misalignment?</span></b><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Although I think this question became quite prominent from </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18397"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MacDiarmid et al. (2025)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I view it as arising from </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.10162"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Denison et al. (2024)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which tested whether models that had learned low-level specification gaming would generalize to the more serious reward tampering when given the chance. They found that it was quite rare. The </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17424"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">original EM paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> then described EM as distinct from reward hacking and sycophancy, citing Denison et al. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This question is significant because there's tons of reward hacking (to some degree due to sloppy RL environments ).</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence for</span></b><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18397"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MacDiarmid et al. (2025)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: misalignment jumped on all evals the moment the model learned to hack. But, the main caveat IMO is that the hacks were primed by synthetic documents or prompts.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14998"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jose &amp; Stastny (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: independent replication of the above on Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct with GRPO. Misalignment rose once hacking started, and runs that never hacked showed no increase. Inoculation prompting blocked it. Again, there were hack hints in prompt. </span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.17511"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Taylor et al. (2025)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: 12% misaligned responses vs 0% for baselines. But, this was in SFT, not RL, so this is proxy evidence for the question.</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06649"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Daniels et al. (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: GPT-4.1 showed covert power-seeking and alignment faking after iterative DPO. This didn’t have synthetic docs or hints, though the task prompts state their own grading criteria, which may make the exploits salient in a similar way. It’s also iterative DPO rather than "full RL", though, the authors appeal to previous work showing iterative DPO approximates GRPO.</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://aclanthology.org/2026.emnlp-main.2151/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jørgenvåg et al. (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: This isn't reward hacking (since the grader openly rewards bad behaviour, so it's arguably in the spirit of the task) but it's relevant to how much we should discount SFT evidence. Here, GRPO produced much more general misalignment than sample-matched SFT, but only did so after some SFT warmup; from scratch, it produced essentially none. So SFT may understate the effect in RL, but when there is misalignment, RL seems to amplify it.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence against (or weak)</span></b><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/reward-seeker/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qi et al. (Anthropic, 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, “Hacker-Opus”. This, IMO, is pretty decisive evidence on an early Opus 4.8 checkpoint with real hackable production environments, and no synthetic documents or hints. It hacked on 40% of episodes! But, it got 0% on all of MacDiarmid et al.’s EM evals, and was as aligned as the starting checkpoint when there was no grader.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fkv5W79rBtAiXqYcK/reward-hacking-without-egregious-misalignment-in-an-rl-only"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudelson et al. (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: This is also clear evidence: it's RL only, on Kimi and GPT-OSS, where similarly, hacking generalized, but EM evals stayed near zero.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/2ANCyejqxfqK2obEj/some-natural-emergent-misalignment-from-reward-hacking-in"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Golechha et al. (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.  The AISI gang primed the hacks with synthetic docs or hints and got consistent hacking. But EM was inconsistent and came mostly from two evals (monitor disruption and framing a colleague). So priming doesn’t seem sufficient on its own, at least on smaller open models.</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/metagaming"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI’s metagaming work (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: in a frontier training run, no misaligned persona and no misbehavior on two of MacDiarmid et al.’s evals. One caveat here is that the model didn't really learn to hack often.</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.19823"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Wang et al. (2025)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: coding reward hacking caused no broad EM, despite seeing increases in deception and oversight sabotage.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Taylor et al. ablation: coding-only hacks produced almost no EM.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is also evidence from</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qi et al, Yudelson et al. and Daniels et al. that it generalises to new forms of reward hacking or reward seeking.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qi et al., Yudelson et al. and Daniels et al. all find that hacking generalises to new forms of reward hacking or reward seeking.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This can be severe. Hacker-Opus tampered with its own reward 41% of the time, gave harmful answers to satisfy a grader 29% of the time, and attacked infrastructure in simulated cyber evals. It only did this when there was a grader or score in play.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This closes the loop with Denison et al.: reward tampering was rare in 2024, and here it’s 41%. So, it's gotten more common as models have gotten better, more RL'd, or something else.</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In terms of complications:</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Inoculation prompting removes the generalization (MacDiarmid et al., Jose &amp; Stastny). But, AISI saw no clear benefit from it.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Synthetic documents framing hacking as acceptable still didn’t prevent EM. Those models ended up more misaligned than prompted ones (Jose &amp; Stastny).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacker-Opus was highly eval-aware on the EM evals. By now it seems the agentic scenarios are too contrived to be reliable.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Judges give some false positives on open models (AISI).</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How should we resolve this tension:</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The starting checkpoint is important. Qi et al think this mattered a lot: their earlier work started from a base model, while Hacker-Opus started from a checkpoint that had already had alignment training.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Priming also matters. EM appears with synthetic documents or hints (MacDiarmid, AISI, Jose &amp; Stastny) and is absent without them (Hacker-Opus, Yudelson). Daniels et al. seems to be kind of a counterexample to both here  but: </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-4.1 is an aligned production model, so Daniels kind of messes with the starting-checkpoint story too.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Daniels et al. cite prior work showing iterative DPO approximates GRPO. Their DPO checkpoints also generalized to agentic reward hacking where the SFT checkpoints didn’t.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In general, I think that the task prompts stating their own grading criteria,  is a kind of salience, and the tasks are mostly single-turn non-code ones.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hack diversity is another candidate here. For example: Hacker-Opus trained on 80 hacks. Taylor et al. needed diverse, non-coding hacks to get EM. Meanwhile, Yudelson et al. used more varied hacks than the AISI replication and got none.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reasoning training might be crucial here (h/t Oliver Daniels). Setups that started from a model without dedicated reasoning training got EM (MacDiarmid et al. (base model), Jose &amp; Stastny (Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct), and Daniels et al. (GPT-4.1, and according to a comment below, Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct-2507 in forthcoming work). On the other hand, setups starting from reasoning models got little or none: Hacker-Opus, Yudelson et al.’s on GPT-OSS and Kimi, OpenAI’s result, and AISI’s GPT-OSS runs. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It can’t be the whole story, though. GPT-OSS and Kimi do become emergently misaligned from SFT (Yudelson et al.), so this would have to be specific to learning hacks on-policy. Speculatively, perhaps reasoning models often rationalise hacks as intended, which may act like self-inoculation.</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="7"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So the answer here is:</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In terms of persona-style EM, you only really get EM reliably when hacking is primed or the start model is weakly aligned. If the model is aligned and unprimed, you don't really get EM (except in the case of Daniels et al). This could also be disambiguated by if the model has reasoning training.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In terms of broader reward seeking: yes, you seem to get it consistently, and it can be severe.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's a missing matched experiment here, as suggested by Claude: {base vs aligned start} × {primed vs unprimed}, on the same environments. Although in general we should score this on both the standard EM evals and probes for </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25891" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conditional misalignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This was written with the intent of being simple and accessible, so it may miss some nuance, with the assistance of Claude Opus 5.5 in collecting papers and directing my attention to experiments.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tn4WSyrqr7ozGLjRy/people-should-resolve-tensions-in-ai-safety-research#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Efficient Lempel-Ziv Prediction with the Run-Length BWT](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/voe2dDnuopYn6DC3N/efficient-lempel-ziv-prediction-with-the-run-length-bwt)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><img alt="Lempel-Ziv arc diagram" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793878636/lexical_client_uploads/gc8xsrmantnkyumqenxj.png" />
<em>The Lempel-Ziv parse of Chad Gadya (חד גדיא), right-to-left, drawn as an arc diagram. Generated by Claude Opus 5.5</em></p>
<p>I have published <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01874">a new paper</a> on efficient string prediction. It was written during the <a href="https://princint.ai/programs/fellowship/">PIBBSS 2026 summer fellowship</a>, under the mentorship of Vanessa Kosoy.</p>
<p>This research builds on <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EEvHYKLsq92LmQ78a/paper-stringological-sequence-prediction-i-1">Kosoy's previous work</a> in stringology. Kosoy built a polynomial-time algorithm for predicting repetitive sequences and I have used other algorithmic techniques to further decrease the runtime.</p>
<p>The goal of this post is to explain some of the ideas from Kosoy's paper and how my work builds on them. My writing will be mostly informal; the field of string algorithms has a lot of technical jargon which I will not try to fully explain. I hope to mostly communicate interesting ideas and topics I've bumped into during the research, while linking to external resources for anyone who wishes to learn more.</p>
<h2>The prediction trinity</h2>
<p>There are three things we want every good predictor to have:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Power</strong>: can predict general, real-world patterns.</li>
<li><strong>Efficiency</strong>: can run in practice.</li>
<li><strong>Formal guarantees</strong>: we can prove what it can or can't do.</li>
</ul>
<p>Unfortunately, no framework today satisfies all three properties at once.</p>
<p>Deep learning is powerful and efficient, but lacks a strong framework of formal guarantees. (This might be a problem!)</p>
<p><a href="https://www.hutter1.net/ai/uaibook2.htm">Solomonoff induction / AIXI</a> are extremely powerful, and have formal guarantees, but can't actually run in practice.</p>
<p>My research is on the third edge: efficient algorithms with formal guarantees, though ones that are still not powerful enough for "actual" intelligence.</p>
<p>Of course, classical learning theory already has plenty of efficient algorithms with provable guarantees (e.g. linear regression). However, these algorithms assume the shape of the hypothesis is fixed in advance and learn its numerical parameters; our algorithms learn compositional structure, as described in Kosoy's <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZwshvqiqCvXPsZEct/the-learning-theoretic-agenda-status-2023#Direction_1__Frugal_Compositional_Languages">compositional learning theory</a>. (Deep learning also learns numerical parameters, but it uses them to represent hidden structure in opaque ways, while our goal is to make such structure explicit.)</p>
<p>The question is then: <strong>which structural prediction tasks can be done efficiently?</strong></p>
<p>The hypothesis space given to Solomonoff induction is extremely ambitious: the environments are generated by <em>any</em> computer program. Solomonoff induction is thus uncomputable and can never be implemented in practice.</p>
<p>We could reduce to bounded-time programs, but it's still too expensive: going over all programs of length  that run in time  takes  time to run. We want prediction time that is at most polynomial in the string's complexity . For that to work, we need a weaker notion of complexity than program length.</p>
<p>So we will work with a very specialized subset of computer programs, called <em>straight-line programs</em>.
Straight-line programs (SLPs) are programs that have two types of instructions:</p>
<ul>
<li>: assign a single symbol  to a variable. ( is a general alphabet but you can think of  as an example)</li>
<li>: define a new string as a concatenation of two previous ones ().</li>
</ul>
<p>An SLP defines strings  in order. The final variable  is the program's output, and  (the number of instructions) is called the size of the program.</p>
<p>We define the <em>Straight-Line Complexity</em> of a string , , to be the size of the smallest SLP that generates .</p>
<p>We write  for the length of a string . We always have : we initialize variables for all symbols in the alphabet, then build the string one symbol at a time by concatenating the next symbol each instruction.</p>
<p>For special strings we can do much better. For example, we can build the <em>Fibonacci words</em><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-1">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> by concatenating the two previous words at each step:</p>
<p></p>
<p>The lengths of  follow the Fibonacci sequence.  grows exponentially in the number of instructions , so these words have very short descriptions: .</p>
<h2>Matching the string's history</h2>
<p>An important property of small SLPs is that the strings they generate tend to be very <em>repetitive</em>. If a small number of instructions is used to generate a large string , it means that the variables  in the program appear over and over again when constructing .</p>
<p>For example, expanding the definition of the Fibonacci word  results in two occurrences of  and three occurrences of : (they actually appear more than that if you look at the boundaries between different terms)</p>
<p></p>
<p>We can use that insight for prediction: when predicting the next symbol, we look at the last few symbols, search for all previous occurrences of this string, and return the symbol that followed them most often.</p>
<p>If we take a partial version of : , we see that the last 4 digits  have two previous occurrences in the string<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-2">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span>, and each such occurrence was followed by a 0. This correctly predicts that the last digit of  is <strong>0</strong>.</p>
<p>The idea of "match the last few symbols" is formalized by the <em>Lempel-Ziv factorization</em> of  (optional technical explanation given below).</p>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Lempel-Ziv factorization</strong></p>
<p>Lempel-Ziv (LZ77) is a string processing algorithm that decomposes a string into repeating components. The Lempel-Ziv phrases  are defined inductively:  is either a single symbol (if that's its first occurrence in ), or the longest prefix of  that has an earlier occurrence in .
 is defined as the number of phrases  in the Lempel-Ziv decomposition of .</p>
<p>The prediction algorithms described in this post are proved in terms of  and not . This suffices for predicting strings generated by small SLPs, because we can show that  in general.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><em>Lempel-Ziv Plurality</em> (LZP) uses the last Lempel-Ziv phrase as its context and predicts the symbol that most often followed its earlier occurrences.</p>
<p>LZP has two operations:</p>
<ul>
<li><code>predict</code>: Output the predicted next symbol,</li>
<li><code>update</code>: Process the actual next symbol after it is revealed.</li>
</ul>
<p>In her paper, Kosoy proves the following result:</p>
<p><strong>Theorem (Kosoy)</strong>: Assume a constant-size alphabet and let  be a string with . Then LZP predicts  with at most  mistakes. A single update and prediction costs .</p>
<p>Now we have an algorithm that runs in polynomial time in terms of the string's complexity !</p>
<h2>Going faster</h2>
<p>What are the conceptual inner workings of LZP? What is it bottlenecked by? Can we make it faster?</p>
<p>LZP provides a reduction: from predicting the <em>future</em> ("what is the next symbol?"), to querying the <em>past</em> ("how often did this substring appear before?"). The problem of string matching is widely studied, and for example, computational biologists use it all the time when searching for short DNA sequences within a genome.</p>
<p>Kosoy uses a data structure by Kempa and Kociumaka called <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.03635"><em>-SA</em></a>, which takes a compressed Lempel-Ziv representation of a string  and "indexes" it to make pattern matching on  much faster. The problem is that it's a <em>static structure</em>: once we get the new symbol , we have to throw away the index we built for  and build a new one for . The amazing property of -SA is that its construction is fast for repetitive strings: the time complexity is , which is polynomial in  and not in  (which can be much larger).</p>
<p>Still, we would like a dynamic structure here.</p>
<p>For this purpose, I use a different string-processing technique called the <strong>Burrows-Wheeler Transform</strong>.</p>
<p>I won't get into the technical details here - <a href="https://www.langmead-lab.org/teaching.html">see here</a> for a great series of lectures on BWT.</p>
<p>The TL;DR is that instead of storing the string  in memory, we store a certain permutation of it, called . Intuitively, characters in  that appear in similar contexts are grouped together in . This permutation has two great properties:</p>
<p><strong>Fast string search:</strong> The occurrences of a string  inside  match a contiguous interval . To find the number of occurrences of  in  (where  is a candidate next symbol),<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-3">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span> we just have to count the number of occurrences of  in . This can be calculated by two <code>rank</code> queries: , where  is the number of occurrences of  in .<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-4">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p><strong>Compressed form:</strong> For repetitive strings ,  consists of a small number of long <em>runs</em> of the same symbol. This allows us to write  much more succinctly, taking less space. This is called the <em>Run-Length</em> representation of BWT, and denoted <em>RLBWT</em> (not to be confused with the other type of RL).</p>
<p>Here's an example adapted from <a href="https://www.cs.jhu.edu/~langmea/resources/lecture_notes/bwt/300_rindex_1_clean.pdf">Langmead's lectures</a>:</p>
<p></p>
<p></p>
<p></p>
<p> has 64 symbols but  only has  runs.</p>
<p><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1910.10631">It can be shown</a> that  in general, so a string with small straight-line complexity will also have a short RLBWT representation.</p>
<p>We can <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1704.05233">implement dynamic RLBWT</a> with efficient <code>update</code> and <code>rank</code> operations: both cost  time, where  is the number of runs in . By the statement above, this is  time.</p>
<p>Using this construction, we get a predictor that:</p>
<ul>
<li>Updates in time ,</li>
<li>Predicts the next symbol in time , where  is the size of the alphabet. (There are  <code>rank</code> queries, two for each candidate symbol.)</li>
</ul>
<p>This is already very efficient: we went down from  to . When the alphabet is small (like ), this is an exponential improvement!</p>
<h2>Going even faster</h2>
<p>But if  is large, prediction is still too slow. What's the new bottleneck? How can we get around it?</p>
<p>If the last Lempel-Ziv phrase of  is , then our task is to find the symbol  that maximizes the number of occurrences of . By our reduction, this is equal to the symbol  that appears most often in an interval . Our RLBWT-based algorithm does this by going over all symbols , calculating the number of occurrences of each one, and choosing the symbol with the most occurrences.</p>
<p>Can we skip the iteration over all symbols?</p>
<p>Let's define a different prediction rule, alternative to LZP, which we'll call <em>Lempel-Ziv Majority</em> (LZM):</p>
<ul>
<li>See if there is a symbol  such that a strict majority of the past occurrences of  in  are followed by .</li>
<li>If there is such a symbol , it is unique; predict .</li>
<li>Else, predict an arbitrary symbol.</li>
</ul>
<p>LZM is a weaker prediction rule than LZP: it doesn't always find the symbol that maximizes the number of occurrences, only when that symbol accounts for over half of all occurrences. Translating back to intervals, the interval  has  as the plurality choice (it appears twice while  each appear once), but  is not a majority (it fills only  of the interval).</p>
<p>However, LZM gives <strong>the same mistake bound</strong> as LZP -  (in fact the proof for LZP's mistake bound extends naturally to LZM). So relaxing the prediction rule doesn't cost us anything in the worst-case.</p>
<p>We do gain a lot in efficiency: now we don't have to calculate the occurrences of <em>all</em> symbols in an interval, only find the majority (if it exists).
We have a more efficient algorithm for doing that, inspired by the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boyer%E2%80%93Moore_majority_vote_algorithm">Boyer-Moore majority algorithm</a>.</p>
<p>Here's a rough idea for how it works (keeping in mind that we are working with the BWT string ):</p>
<p>Take the substring , and at each step take a pair of distinct symbols  that appear next to each other in , and make both disappear. Repeat this process until nothing remains, or until  has only one type of symbol left. If there is a strict majority , it is guaranteed to be the only symbol remaining.</p>
<p>Cancelling the symbols one step at a time still takes  steps, which is  in the worst-case. If we use the run-length representation to cancel entire runs, this reduces to . If in addition we use a binary tree that stores each run in a node, and precalculate the cancellations of many intervals in , this reduces to  time (the height of the tree).</p>
<p>This results in a predictor that supports both updates and predictions in time , regardless of the alphabet size.</p>
<h2>Going fastest</h2>
<p>Yet, for small alphabets, we can do better.</p>
<p>To see how, we need to ask which computational model we are using.</p>
<p>For a lot of computational problems, this question doesn't matter: all types of computation are equivalent, and any computational model that's powerful enough can simulate any other model. Turing machines are the classic example, but since we can <a href="https://efeozbatur.com/minecraft-is-a-turing-complete-video-game/">build a computer in Minecraft</a>, the halting problem is just as relevant for redstone.</p>
<p>However, these different models do differ in terms of efficiency. Turing machines only read and write one bit at a time.
Multiplying two 64-bit integers naively takes about  bit-operations. But in modern CPUs, this is done by a single assembly instruction.</p>
<p>The <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word_RAM">word RAM model</a> is a way to formalize that. The basic unit of memory is a -bit word, and we are allowed to add, multiply and do other basic operations with these words in a single instruction.
CPUs often use , but we can abstract away to any value of : the larger the word-size gets, the more powerful our machine becomes, and it is useful to consider how efficient our algorithms get for different values of .</p>
<p>Allowing fast arithmetic on -bit integers uncovers a new toolbox of efficient operations.</p>
<p>One example is parallel comparison (explanation adapted from the <a href="https://web.stanford.edu/class/archive/cs/cs166/cs166.1256/lectures/17/">Stanford lecture notes</a>). Assume we have 8 pairs of 7-bit integers that we want to compare, that is, answer the following 8 queries:
</p>
<p>Pack  into one 64-bit word . Each number  takes up the bottom 7 bits of a byte; we'll set the remaining top bits to <strong>1</strong>.
Similarly, pack  into one 64-bit word , and set the top bit in each byte to <strong>0</strong>.
Then subtract  from :</p>
<p></p>
<p>The th byte computes , so there is no borrowing between bytes. Its top bit gives the answer:</p>
<p></p>
<p>One subtraction achieves eight comparisons.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-5">
      <sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span> A similar trick is <a href="https://github.com/lattera/glibc/blob/master/string/strlen.c">implemented in glibc</a> for testing several bytes of a string at once.</p>
<p>In the RAM model, we can think of a machine word as a miniature database. It can be used to store many small values in a packed data structure, and using additions and multiplications, we can encode many clever operations on these values. Parallel comparison is just one example of many. (For another example, an array of  integers <a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S019667740300155X">can be sorted in  time</a>, beating the usual  lower bound for comparison-based sorting.)</p>
<p>Bille et al.'s <a href="https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00453-017-0380-7">dynamic partial-sums structure</a> uses such word-RAM techniques. Using it in our LZP predictor, we get a time of  per symbol.</p>
<p>We need to fix the word size  ahead of time. What value of  do we use?</p>
<p>Assume the final string length  is known ahead of time. If , this means that the number  doesn't fit in a single machine word. This would make index calculations in  expensive - it would take multiple operations just to calculate the difference between two offsets in , for example. Thus, a reasonable choice is .</p>
<p>Another way to think of it is first choosing , then limiting the string length up to . (For comparison,  allows up to  symbols - this is 18 Exabytes, likely more than the training data for all frontier models.  supports much more data than humanity ever generated.)</p>
<p>So, choosing , we get a running time of .
In particular, there are many highly-repetitive strings which satisfy  for some constant . Fibonacci words are an example for  and the constant alphabet . For these strings, all updates and predictions <strong>always run in constant time</strong>, .</p>
<p>In fact, I believe that we can do even better than that - a construction giving  time per symbol. This would result in constant-time operations for , even for large alphabets. I also believe this runtime is asymptotically optimal for the "incremental RLE" data structure I use in my paper. The construction and proofs were generated with AI, and I haven't fully checked them yet, so I've left these last claims out of the paper.</p>
<h2>Ending thoughts</h2>
<p>The predictors described here, while interesting in theory, are clearly not sufficient for designing safe AI systems. These are all toy models that are not nearly powerful enough to compete with modern ML algorithms.</p>
<p>I think of it as a proof of concept for future research: given a tractable prediction goal, we can make tailor-made, highly optimized implementations that achieve this goal.</p>
<p>AI is already being used to improve the efficiency of its next generation. While my type of research feels similar to capabilities research in some ways (improving the runtime of algorithms), its output is an algorithm that we could fully understand and have formal guarantees on.</p>
<p>My ultimate goal is to have an AI that is built, not grown. We are obviously very far away from that point, and it is likely that the gap between verifiable and heuristic AI won't be closed in time absent a significant pause or slowdown. I still think researchers should put significant effort into formal methods. Optimizing LLMs all the way to the endgame is not an inevitable outcome, and advances in other, safer forms of intelligence could make all the difference.</p>
<p><em>Thanks to Vanessa for helpful suggestions on an earlier draft of this post.</em></p>

I chose a different convention for aesthetic reasons but the resulting structure is the same. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-2"><p>I've chosen the last four symbols for clarity, but the actual algorithm would use a different context size. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-3"><p>We actually use  throughout the algorithm instead of . We have to reverse the string, because BWT allows us to extend string queries on the <em>left</em> ( to ), not on the right. This is called a <em>backward step</em>. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-4"><p>We often define intervals as half-open, , and define  as the number of occurrences of  <em>before</em> position . The count is then . Apologies for any off-by-one confusions that will surely arise! <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-5"><p>Besides subtraction, we need to prepare the input (pack the values  into  and the values  into ), and isolate the output (use the top bits as inputs to other functions). This incurs additional operations, but they are done in constant time as well. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-6tBeGtYToHe7bxNxE-5">↩︎</a></p>

### [Useful Junk](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dYZrtZYty2CSCLjJS/useful-junk)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>

Occasionally people will get excited about a minimalism fad, where you
figure out which of your possessions you need and keep only those.  As
someone who generally doesn't like getting rid of things, I've been
thinking some about why, and the main thing I keep coming back to is
that random objects can be helpful in unpredictable ways.  Here are
some things that I kept without a specific use in mind, but that ended
up being useful.  You could say they saved time or money, but often
the biggest gain was from being able to do a project in one go with
what I already had on hand:

</span>

</p><p>

</p>

<ul>

<li><p>Ethernet cable.  Back in 2019 if you'd asked me whether I would
ever use ethernet again, I would have said probably not.  WiFi was
good enough for everything I was doing.  But then I (like a lot of us)
suddenly started working from home and got frustrated about how poorly
my internet was working <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/ethernet-is-worth-it-for-video-calls">for video calls</a> and
ran ethernet to my desk.  Having cable on hand already meant I could
just set it up.</p></li>

<li><p>Plexiglass.  When I built my <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/accidental-electronic-instrument">accidental
electronic instrument</a> I wanted to protect it, and had plexiglass
left over from when I added an insulating layer to a single-pane
window.</p></li>

<li>
<p>Scrap wood.  So many things.  To give one example, when one of
the stairs in our basement fell apart, I was able to replace it with
extras from past projects that I'd kept around.

</p>
<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/repaired-basement-stairs-big.jpg"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/dYZrtZYty2CSCLjJS/hlawsuu5wvpreynvnlw3" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>
<p>

Similarly, when Lily came back from visiting a friend enamored of
their prop axe, she had everything she needed to make one already in
the basement:

</p>
<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/prop-axe-big.jpg"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/dYZrtZYty2CSCLjJS/covzycxgg9ogtrdgwnhk" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>
</li>

<li><p>Computer mouse.  One of my housemates was having wrist
issues, and wanted to try out using a <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/floor-mouse">mouse
on the floor</a>.  They ended up hating it after a day or so, but
being able to pull one out of the junk electronics pile and try it
made for quick experimentation.</p></li>

<li><p>Plastic sheeting.  When we wanted to divide our apartment in
two so we could <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/adding-a-housemate-under-covid">isolate a
new housemate</a> I had sheeting left over from an earlier project.</p></li>

<li><p>Screen.  When I wanted to make a screen door for our <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/kid-door">kid door</a> I already had window screen lying
around.</p></li>

<li>
<p>Hinge.  Lily wanted to make a "laptop" for Anna as a present,
and I had an old hinge lying around from who-knows-what that was just
right for having it stay open at the right angle.  Here's the laptop
after Anna decorated it:

</p>
<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/anna-wooden-laptop-open-big.jpg"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/dYZrtZYty2CSCLjJS/cp1ywtpzj1x9xi9sa8ez" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>
<p>

And with the hinge it closes just right:

</p>
<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/anna-wooden-laptop-closed-big.jpg"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/dYZrtZYty2CSCLjJS/jyigv808dgsbeqjqdt0q" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>
</li>
<li>
<p>Volleyball net.  When we went back to the vacation house where
Nora had a <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/shallow-water-is-dangerous-too">close
call</a> with a fountain, we wanted to put up a barrier.  The kids
would probably have been fine without, but we wanted something that
would clearly mark it as out of bounds. We got some chicken wire, but
realized when we were packing that it was only about half as long as
we needed. An old volleyball net made up the difference:

</p>
<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/duck-pond-volleyball-net-fence-big.jpg"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/dYZrtZYty2CSCLjJS/gnlwhqdpaqwmigvqrdzy" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>
</li>
<li>
<p>Hand saw.  I would have said there's no situation in which a
traditional manual crosscut saw is the best tool for the job, but when
the city cut down the tree in front of our house for being diseased,
Lily wanted a piece of the stump to remember it by. She really wanted
to cut it off herself, without assistance from electricity or other
humans. Luckily, I still had the tool for that:

</p>
<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/lily-stump-cutting-big.jpg"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/dYZrtZYty2CSCLjJS/rjsizoivihxoozltavll" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>
<p>

She did change her mind on help after realizing how hard it was,
though I don't think Nora was very helpful:

</p>
<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/nora-tree-cutting-big.jpg"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/dYZrtZYty2CSCLjJS/k5pyf0nuesx3mnemp5b8" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>
</li>
</ul>



<p>

Having a wide range of objects in the basement is something I find
useful on a regular basis.  One thing I like about projects is that
they often leave me with interesting scraps I can use for other
things.  I especially like that they're not very valuable, because
that means I feel much more free to play with them.

</p>

<p>

If we were moving often, or didn't have a large unfinished space, of
course, the tradeoff would be different.  And many things go into the
basement never to be used again (so far!)  Since we do have the space,
though, keeping things without knowing what we might need them for
seems well worth it.

  </p>

### [The Data Product Platform Navigator](https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Many organizations adopting data mesh and data products face a common
     challenge. They may be tasked with building a self-service platform for
     data products and data mesh from scratch, or they may have an existing
     platform that suffers from low adoption, preventing their data mesh from
     scaling. This article provides a platform navigator, based on the CNCF's
     Platform Engineering Maturity Model, to help you reason about your current
     situation and determine the next steps for your platform.</p>
