### [Bliki: LLM Voice](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Sometimes when I read a post, I get the growing feeling that I'm reading
  the output of an LLM. I can't necessarily define why, although there are the common
  tells (it's not the em-dashes, it's the load-bearing tapestries). As I
  do this my desire to read on atrophies, sometimes to the point of revulsion</p>

<p>There are tools out there that claim to be able to tell the difference
  between LLM-written prose and the old-fashioned biological product. I'm wary
  of them, partly due skeptism about their accuracy, but also because it rather
  misses the point. The reaction to LLM-Voice is a human one, that sense that we
  are in an <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncanny_valley">uncanny
  valley</a> of prose. By the time I'd reach for a tool to tell me what I'm
  reading, it's already too late, even if it confidently tells me there's no LLM
  present.</p>

<p>I'm also suspicious of my own ability to accurately recognize LLM voice. Language
  changes over time, so a common sign of age is complaining about the language
  of the youngs. I'm inclined to bristle at prose that talks about “adopting a
  new sales <i>motion</i>,” or “a <i>sharp</i> round-up of news”. But I've heard
  these constructs coming out of human mouths, so that's more a generational
  difference rather than between carbon and silicon. (And as a transplanted Brit,
  don't get me started on Americanisms, I'm sure I'll get used to them momentarily.)</p>

<p>Right now I urge authors to be extra-wary of LLM-Voice. In particular I
  strongly recommend that authors should not fall into the trap of getting the
  LLM to write a draft. Even if it's edited by a human, it's very hard to expel
  the LLM-Voice - thus causing the uncanny valley reaction. 
<sup>1</sup>
 Even accepting suggestions of rephrasing is dangerous. I
  have heard of people using LLMs to develop outline and structure, that
  <i>may</i> be ok, but only as far as an outline. 
<sup>2</sup>

  Authors should fill the prose themselves. </p>

<p><a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SayYourWriting.html">Say Your Writing</a> is
  even more important in the age of LLMs. I habitually speak the words in my head
  and then write them down (and then edit what I've just written). This advice
  is particularly important to anyone who has any desire to communicate
  regularly with writing. Like many things, learning to be a good writer relies
  on lots of practice. It's a skill worth the time to acquire, but surrendering
  to an LLM will undermine any progress.</p>

<p>In time it may be that LLMs will be so good that we can't tell the
  difference. In many ways, that's more worrying. Like most writers, I find
  writing an almost-essential tool for thinking. Often it's only by writing
  something down, that I can really get my thoughts on a topic into coherence.
  I've often started an essay knowing I don't really understand something, but
  the act of wrestling with the words forces me to overcome my confusions. As
  more people delegate writing to LLMs they will also delegate thinking and
  comprehension to LLMs. I don't want to do that, where's the fun of writing then?</p>

<p>Although avoiding LLM-Voice is a general rule, it isn't an absolute one. An
  important example is that of Jim Highsmith (who contributes to this site). He
  finds working with an LLM is essential to his writing to <a href="https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/60/9/677">overcome the bane of Parkinson's
  disease</a>. I'll gladly deal with LLM-Voice in order to continue imbibing my
  old friend's wisdom.</p>

<p>A more awkward case is that of translation. Many of my colleagues aren't
  comfortable in English, it is their second or fourth language. Some have found
  LLM translation an important part to their communication with those of us who
  know rather less Marathi. But I also would prefer rather a genuine human's clunky prose to the
  smooth but soulless LLM.</p>

<p>There are situations where it would be reasonable for even a native
  Englishman to let LLMs write the prose. Such as if I'm writing a form letter
  to an official, or preparing a report I suspect no-one will ever read 
<sup>3</sup>
. Maybe
  most documentation in the future will only be read by LLMs, and they won't
  have my uncanny valley worries. But if I want to communicate with humans, my
  humanity has to shine (or at least ooze) out of my prose.</p>

<div class="footnote-list">
<h2>Notes</h2>

<div class="footnote-list-item" id="footnote-impossible">
<p><span class="num">1: </span>
      Very hard, but not impossible. I've come across one case where I do know
      the first draft was entirely done by LLM, but I detected no LLM Voice when
      I read the final piece. It did, however, need a professional editor at a
      major periodical publication to clean it up.
    </p>
</div>

<div class="footnote-list-item" id="footnote-policy">
<p><span class="num">2: </span>
      My policy on this site, is that anyone who uses AI to help them write
       should declare how they used it, usually in the
      acknowledgments. This is partly for transparency, and partly so that
      readers can learn useful ways to use AI. In my own case, I don't use AI
      for generating prose, nor for help in structure. The only way I use it for
      writing is as a search tool (but I verify any information by going
      to the source).
    </p>
</div>

<div class="footnote-list-item" id="footnote-official">
<p><span class="num">3: </span>
      Michael Nygard commented: “There you really aren't
      trying to communicate thoughts from one person to other people. You're
      communicating with a process rather than a human. A human may open the
      envelope but that person is acting as a component in a cybernetic
      organism.”
    </p>
</div>
</div>

<div class="acknowledgements">
<h2>Acknowledgements</h2>

<p>Alessio Ferri, Caer Sanders, Ian Cartwright, Jie Jessie Xia, Joshua
    Kerievsky, Michael Feathers, Michael Nygard, Scott Davies, and Wei Zhang commented on
    drafts of this post.</p>

<p>I did not use AI or LLMs in any way while writing this post.</p>
</div>

---

### [Why are good developers so wary of agentic development?](https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/good-devs-wary.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p class="precis"><b>TL;DR</b><br /><i>Good developers’ caution about agentic development comes from knowing that built isn’t the same as working in production.</i></p>
<img src="https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/card.png" /><p>I’ve been surprised by how many excellent developers have been hesitant to go all in on coding agents. These are people who usually try new ways of working before anyone else and in many cases invent new tools and practices that support building the application well. So why have we had so much reluctance?</p>

<p>The easy (lazy?) answer is that they love writing code, or they’re worried about their jobs. I’m sure that’s true for some people, some of the time. And I know it is true for some of the developers I talk to but I always felt there was something else, another fear hiding behind those obvious ones. When we put together the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/en-us/radar">Technology Radar</a> every six months I try to block out a chunk of time for us to do an open space. In September, a discussion we had on agentic development, which pushed it to its limits, finally helped me see clearly what that fear is.</p>

<h2 id="building-it-right">Building it right</h2>

<p>Thoughtworkers care deeply about <em>building the right thing and building it right</em>. It’s pretty much our default way of working and thinking and it’s where many of the practices and technologies we invented and championed came from. We want software that is useful and helpful, can be changed next year, and doesn’t fall over in production. Our developers adopted, championed or invented some of the first versions of continuous integration, pairing, automated testing, continuous delivery, microservices and many others early because they helped us build the software right.</p>

<p>Early coding agents made it very easy to produce code, including code we wouldn’t have wanted anywhere near production, written by people who have not learned about the chasm between building something and making it run in production safely. The tools have improved enormously, but experienced developers still have questions. Are we confident this is the right behaviour? Does it work as expected? Are these the right boundaries? Will we be able to evolve it? Does it abide by the principles and practices we learned the hard way to give us confidence it will meet the needs of the customer and stay up in production? What happens when something fails? Will another team be able to live with it? And so on.</p>

<p>Underneath all of those sits a bigger question. <strong>How much can we trust a system when nobody has experience of building it this way?</strong> Let me state that another way. Nearly every system we have in production now was built the old way, by hand, by people we could trust (hopefully). Everything we build now is the new way and we have not lived with that yet.</p>

<p>After more than 25 years in technology, I’ve come to value the judgment people develop through experience. I regularly put similar questions to models and to senior technologists at Thoughtworks. For the difficult questions, I still get more useful answers from the humans or maybe more insightful with very specific experience that helps me better understand the point. They challenge the premise, recognise a pattern from something that went wrong ten years ago, or spot the organisational problem hiding inside the technical one. I can now access an extraordinary amount of information. I still need people who can help me think through what matters, I still need wisdom and insight. That type judgment is exactly what hesitant experienced developers are trying to protect.</p>

<p>And I want to be clear: they are not hesitant to use a coding assistant, they are hesitant to go all in and let the agents do all the execution. They are hesitant to take their hand off the wheel because they don’t have full confidence the agents have the right guardrails in place to run off on their own.</p>

<h2 id="a-week-at-the-whiteboard">A week at the whiteboard</h2>

<p>Recently, Giles Alexander, our Regional CTO for Europe, brought senior engineers, infrastructure consultants and product people together for a week of working in exactly this way. No code written by anyone. Everything built by agents. The point wasn’t to prove that an airline application could be built in a week. (Funnily enough they got 80% done and we all know what that means… you can’t make stuff up!) The purpose of the experiment was to learn what happens to the team’s work when agents do all of the coding. You can read some of what they learned <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/an-accidental-blackboard.html">here</a>.</p>

<p>What struck me in our conversations was how much time they spent at the whiteboard. Working out the domain. Drawing boundaries. Deciding what should happen next and making plans the agents could act on. Meanwhile, the agents kept producing code beyond the team’s working day.</p>

<p>The humans were also designing the feedback around that work: tests, contracts, architecture checks and a delivery pipeline. They were working out how to give agents enough freedom to make progress while keeping confidence in the result. Most of the work at the keyboard was directing agents to put this learning into the harness, so the building and testing agents got it right on the next run and they could have more confidence in what they built. Interestingly in the end the product person committed more feature code than anyone else on the team.</p>

<p>When I asked them to estimate they said they spend about ⅔ of their time at the whiteboard and ⅓ on the harness, which could give us some clue as to how developers will work in the future, but before you get ahead of yourself redesigning your team, that’s not the end of the story…</p>

<h2 id="the-application-ran-the-build-was-red">The application ran. The build was red.</h2>

<p>The application ran, but the build was frequently red. It worked on their machine - again you couldn’t make this up if you tried! A constant red build gives me the ick. A red build should mean something: stop, investigate, fix it. If it’s red so often that people stop paying attention, how do we spot the next real failure? And if we can’t trust the checks, a green build stops being reassuring too. That’s exactly the uncertainty <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/continuousIntegration.html">continuous integration</a> was supposed to remove, and we’ve been advocating it for over 25 years.</p>

<p>We’re still working through how to address it. Remember this was an experiment to push the very edge of agentic development. Were too many agents working in parallel and stepping on each other’s changes? Was the system not yet modular enough for agents to work on separate parts? Or were changes simply arriving faster than the checks could finish? Or was it a combination of all of these and more? Either way we definitely need to rethink how we will solve the last mile challenges. I can see continuous delivery/DevOps as a movement having a resurgence and a rethink in the agentic era in the same way the rest of the development life cycle is being upended. And it isn’t just our experiment. <a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/ai-machine-learning/announcing-the-2025-dora-report?utm_source=chatgpt.com">DORA’s 2025</a> research found AI adoption is now linked to higher delivery throughput, but to lower delivery stability. Exactly what continuous delivery and DevOps were trying to solve for!</p>

<p>All of these challenges prompted some good arguments internally. What role should test-driven development play when you aren’t reading every line and the agent created the tests alongside the code? Where would acceptance tests, contract tests and checks at system boundaries give us more confidence without being too brittle? How much design should we do before setting an agent loose, given how quickly we can revise an approach? Is there an appropriate point we will know we have gone too far? One of the biggest puzzles in all this is the size of the batch. I have heard many teams are moving from Stories to Epics. Great for speed of development but can your CI pipeline handle that size unit of work at speed and in parallel?</p>

<p>The experiment team didn’t settle all of this in a week or even two (they ran it twice with different teams). I wouldn’t trust anyone who claimed they had. But this is exactly the kind of work I want experienced developers involved in: experimenting, questioning and helping us discover which practices hold up and where we need some new ones.</p>

<h2 id="off-the-groomed-slopes">Off the groomed slopes</h2>

<p>After coming away from this conversation with some of the senior developers involved I kept thinking about learning to ski. Kids will often head straight down the hill without fear, kind of like a new builder who has never put software into production and has never fixed an on-call issue at 3am (something I will never forget, right up there with every production issue I personally caused and the resultant learnings from those, which are burned in my brain forever). When I first tried to ski as an adult, I was thinking about what I might break. I wanted to ski, it looked so fun and freeing, but I was old enough to see the consequences and I didn’t fancy hobbling around London with a broken limb or worse. My biggest issue was I hadn’t yet learned how to turn or stop effectively and without that I was a runaway train on a ski slope.</p>

<p>When I shared a draft of this post with Martin, who also learned to ski late, he pointed out where the comparison breaks down (like all analogies I like to push them to the edge, pun intended). On a ski slope you’ve watched countless people reach the bottom with no broken bones. The instructors have taught thousands of people before you on the same slope. They know which skills matter and how to teach them. Agentic development right now is more like a backcountry trip across unknown territory. We don’t yet know which skills will matter most. Not many people, if any, have finished the trip safely and the feedback on how to navigate it is slow or non-existent. As Martin put it, we won’t know whether a heavily agent-built system works until it has run in production for a while, survived a few rounds of change, and not sent all our planes to Aberystwyth. <strong>Built is not the same as working in production.</strong> Or as Charity Majors <a href="https://x.com/mipsytipsy/status/1822877010894758096">puts it</a>, “staging is just a glorified laptop. Only prod is prod”. Which is why when someone says “it works on my machine” it’s usually intended as a joke. Or at least I always laugh.</p>

<p>That solidified a nagging discomfort I have had with leaders, technical or non-technical, claiming devs aren’t curious, or are laggards slow to adopt, and putting that down to easy-to-point-at fears like missing writing code (I do too) and fearing for our jobs (who isn’t right now?). Our best developers aren’t nervous beginners on a nursery slope that everyone else has already mastered. They’re looking at unmarked terrain and asking sensible questions about the route. Telling them to be less cautious misses the point. They need to be on the team that maps it.</p>

<p>Of course, experience can also make us too attached to familiar practices. Some of what we learned on the groomed slopes won’t hold out there, and we need to be willing to test our assumptions.</p>

<h2 id="a-challenge-for-both-sides">A challenge for both sides</h2>

<p>So there’s a challenge here for both leaders and developers. Leaders need to listen carefully enough to tell useful concerns from reluctance to change, and to create real room for experimentation. That means time, permission to get things wrong, and not judging success by how much code the agents produced. Developers need to bring their experience into those experiments and be prepared to find that some of their assumptions no longer hold.</p>

<p>Some familiar practices will become more important. Others will need a different shape. Nobody is going to hand us a map first.</p>

<p>If you care deeply about building the right thing and building it right, your judgment is exactly what this new way of working needs. Bring the caution. Then let’s set off into the snow.</p>

---

### [Fragments: October 28](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-28.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Andrej Karpathy sees <a href="https://x.com/karpathy/status/2109361546505966046">a widening gap</a> in how people see LLM capabilities.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The general public has mostly not interacted with these systems. When they have, it looks like a derpy chatbot. The majority of professionals still see only a modest uplift. And a small sliver of professionals are experiencing the vertigo of the curve going vertical. And it is all happening at the same time.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Many of my colleagues are living in a world where LLM capabilities are stunning, but it’s easy to forget that’s not what most people experience. One thing I do is use free-tier LLM chatbots, and I’m still finding that while most of the time they are useful, their unreliability undermines much of that usefulness. Yes, you can argue that I’m not using frontier tools or a forceful enough harness, but that’s true of most regular folks too. To understand their perception, it’s handy to experience it.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>That observation particularly resonates with me today, as I’ve just spent the weekend at  Foocamp, an <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/OpenSpace.html">open space</a> event run by O’Reilly. It was full of those who are experiencing that vertigo of LLM capabilities, so it felt very much like being in a bubble of those on the bleeding edge. While it’s worth remembering that that’s not a typical view of what’s happening, it’s good to see what those explorers are experiencing. (As I talk about this, I’m applying the <a href="https://www.chathamhouse.org/about-us/chatham-house-rule">Chatham House rule</a>, if anyone wants attribution, let me know.)</p>

<p>A couple of folks talked about doing immersion courses to help teach developers how to do <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">agentic engineering</a>. The idea is to get people out of their usual work environment, and spend one or two weeks with experienced coaches, learning how to use these tools well. Such things remind me very much of the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/ExtremeProgramming.html">Extreme Programming</a> immersion classes I was involved with at the turn of the century. As with that, the experience of building something is important to understand such a new style of work.</p>

<p>Unsurprisingly the coaches observed that the biggest impact wasn’t technical, but emotional. New tools are just new tools, but <em>these</em> tools change basic assumptions of what it means to be a software developer, which for many people challenges their identity. One coach said how his main job was to take such people out for a walk to a coffee shop, so they could process what’s happening. (This reminds me of a participant of the Extreme Programming immersion who was so horrified by the use of a non-locking version-control system (CVS) that he walked out of the class on the first day.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’m wary of talking about themes to an event where I attend less than one-tenth of the sessions, but a topic I kept coming across was how large enterprises were embracing this technology - or rather not. One participant used a lovely metaphor: corporations have acquired a Swiss-army knife, but are only using the toothpick. I see plenty of decent reasons for this: it’s one thing for a startup or individual to use a hot technology, but there’s a big difference between a website going down and your factories grinding to a halt. Big organizations involve workflows involving lots of people that have built up over many years - they don’t change easily. Security concerns abound, the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#lethal-trifecta">lethal trifecta</a> still lurks.</p>

<p>In particular good use of AI in enterprises relies on good data, and many such organizations don’t have their data in a decent shape. The <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html">path to getting agent-ready data</a> isn’t trivial and it’s hard to see how we can get the knife and saw out until enterprises are much further down that path. One thing that struck me is that there was little conversation about this at Foocamp - a sign of its visibility among the people there.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I very much enjoyed Foocamp, but events like this are always a mix of excitement and dread. I knew very few of the people there, so it was a great opportunity to get out of my own social bubble. But I also find it nearly impossible to walk up to people I don’t know (or know slightly) and impose my company on them. The great thing about unconferences is that the nature of the sessions make it plausible for someone like me to engage in conversation during them, and begin some tentative links. Also I thank the many folks there who were friendly to me as I casually walked around trying to hide how much I was panicking.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Laurie Voss says <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/apollo-says-ai-hasnt-paid-off-outside-tech-thats-because-laurie-voss-pkotc/">today is 1997</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>On Saturday, Apollo’s chief economist Torsten Slok published No Signs of AI Payoff Outside the Tech Sector. It’s short and it’s blunt: outside of 7 major tech companies, there is no data yet to support a productivity gain that justifies all the money pouring into tech.</p>

  <p>My position is that he’s half right: are valuations too high? Absolutely. We’re in a bubble, and it’s absolutely going to pop. But does that mean investing in AI is a waste of money? Absolutely not, and using this early data to try and decide that question would be like trying to decide in 1997 whether the Internet was going to pay off. The data just isn’t there yet, because these things take time.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I’m not as sure as he is that we can peg today to 1997 rather than 1999, but I agree that this boom bears a lot of resemblance to the dotcom boom (although I think this one is bigger). While many dotcom companies crashed (I always think of pets.com and webvan) he’s right to point out that anyone who invested in Amazon in 1997 did very well. Where might those good investments be this time?</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>if you can build something that proves you got the answer, AI can get the answer much faster than you can, and there are real productivity gains to be had doing that generally.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>As we think about this, remember that investment isn’t just buying shares, but also where we choose to invest that precious resource of our time and energy.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Adam Tornhill asks <a href="https://adamtornhill.substack.com/p/were-all-those-years-of-coding-wasted">Were All Those Years of Coding Wasted?</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>For me, agentic coding is enjoyable because it lets me take on larger parts of a problem than I would have been able to master manually. Infrastructure updates are no longer a blocker. And I don’t need to spend time learning about a build system I never cared about.</p>

  <p>My personal goal is to get better at working with higher-level abstractions, and accept that writing code is likely to be a dying skill. My bet on the future is that our problem solving skills, logical mindset, and understanding of systems will be at least as rewarding.</p>

  <p>In the end, we only need to challenge our identity if we mistake programming for writing code. AI finally allows us to separate the two. And that’s where all those years of experience transfer the best: knowing not only what to build but how to build it well.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Tim Bray <a href="https://www.tbray.org/ongoing/When/202x/2026/10/19/The-AI-Temptation">looks at this from another direction</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>For me (and I’m not alone), the biggest issue isn’t what GenAI can and can’t do, it’s the ethical and economic discomforts. This technology is being peddled by terrible people who are rich and whose goal is to get richer. It’s bought and deployed by terrible people who want to slash their payrolls by throwing thought workers onto the street. It facilitates the oncoming climate catastrophe and devalues human creative effort.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Annie Vella observes that the change we’re seeing in software development follows a way of thinking about automation that goes back to the 1960’s as the distinction between  <a href="https://annievella.com/posts/supervisory-engineering-work/">direct and supervisory control</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The main difference between direct and supervisory control is that in the latter, there’s a computer that closes the loop instead of a human operator. Before AI, writing software was much more like direct control - we closed the loop ourselves. We interpreted requirements, made changes to the code, received feedback and acted on it, over and over. Now, coding agents can increasingly close that loop themselves - writing code, running tests and fixing their own mistakes - while we move towards supervisory control: telling the agent what we want, keeping an eye on how it’s going and taking over when we need to.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>She outlines various ideas that this intellectual thread developed. The five-level model developed by Endsley and Kiris seems apposite:</p>

<table>
  <thead>
    <tr>
      <th>Level</th>
      <th>Human</th>
      <th>System</th>
      <th>In AI-assisted software development</th>
    </tr>
  </thead>
  <tbody>
    <tr>
      <td>1. None</td>
      <td>Decide, act</td>
      <td>-</td>
      <td>No AI assistance</td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td>2. Decision support</td>
      <td>Decide, act</td>
      <td>Suggest</td>
      <td>The early days of AI coding assistants’ autocomplete</td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td>3. Consensual AI</td>
      <td>Concur</td>
      <td>Decide, act</td>
      <td>AI coding agents where you must approve the actions they take</td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td>4. Monitored AI</td>
      <td>Veto</td>
      <td>Decide, act</td>
      <td>Auto-mode, with the ability to veto the coding agent’s actions</td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td>5. Full automation</td>
      <td>-</td>
      <td>Decide, act</td>
      <td>Full auto-mode, such as might be used in a dark software factory</td>
    </tr>
  </tbody>
</table>

<p>Vella cautions that this comes with an important caveat:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There is one notable difference between the automation described by this prior research and the type of automation that AI agents give us. Both sets of levels assume that the computer’s account of what it did is accurate and honest. As we all know by now, what an AI agent says it did and what it actually did may not be the same thing. Still, it’s automation, and it still requires supervision - arguably more, since we can’t simply take its word for it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Vella’s conclusion from all this is that: “our next step must be to start redesigning the job and the system, in tandem”. I agree with this, and the Foocampers’ immersion courses I mentioned earlier are part of that exercise. The tricky thing is that we don’t yet know what this new job design looks like, but I think Vella is on the right path when she says:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What if we stopped thinking of the SDLC as a process we follow, and instead started thinking of it as a control system we build and supervise?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>A while ago I mentioned how much I enjoyed Eric Evans’s keynote at DDD Europe. Now the <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iZ2-Nzdsnb4">video is available</a> so those who weren’t in Antwerp can watch it.</p>

<p>He opens by looking at our future, saying that rather than fruitlessly trying to predict it, we should instead explore it with “curiosity, fun, and wonder”. He’s been doing this, digging deeper into the theory and fundamentals that underpin AI.</p>

<p>He goes into detail on his side project: the Domain Language Surveyor. The idea is to be able to examine different repositories, and analyze the vocabulary used inside those repositories to see if they are related <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/BoundedContext.html">bounded contexts</a>. To do this he built a pipeline of tools, illustrating how a useful system blends both computational and inferential components. He then used this pipeline to analyze the vocabulary of some open-source healthcare codebases, to see how their bounded contexts related.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>A topic that regularly comes up when I talk to companies outside the Foocamp world, is the cost of tokens, and the concern about how that cost is going to increase in the future. We’ve been worrying about that at Thoughtworks, and my colleague Andy Nolan describes our <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/blog/generative-ai/renting-the-frontier--owning-the-core-ip--how-we-cut-ai-forecast">token sourcing strategy</a>, which we forecast will reduce our token costs in 2028 from $16-40M  down to $6.8-1M.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The strategy rents frontier models for the work that needs frontier-level reasoning. It runs open-weight models, on capacity we choose, for the work that carries our IP or runs at a volume where owning the capacity costs less.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The strategy involves looking at each LLM workload, and classifying it on two axes: domain differentiation and security &amp; sovereignty. We then place each workload on a nine cell grid.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The nine cells run from the cheapest capable API, swapped as prices change, to a fine-tuned open model on our own infrastructure, with full control and full upkeep. For a given workload, the highest score on a given axis determines the right position in the grid. There is no single right place for all workloads. Each organization ends up with its own distribution and mix of workloads across the grid.</p>
</blockquote>

<p><img alt=" " src="https://www.thoughtworks.com/content/dam/thoughtworks/documents/blog/machine-learning-and-ai/tw_blog_token_sourcing_image2.png" /></p>

---

### [Fragments: October 21](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-21.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Gergely Orosz is frustrated at those that entered his explicitly “No AI” essay competition and used AI. He <a href="https://x.com/GergelyOrosz/status/2108863325756961274">goes on to say:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>When I start to suspect some part is AI about someone I don’t know: how can I tell how much of it is? And how much time and effort do I want to invest to figure out?</p>

  <p>I fear most people do not realize this last part: that when it takes time and effort from others to figure out what % is AI, and what % is YOU, then I will simply assume it’s probably 100% AI and 0% you</p>

  <p>So when producing anything that is meant for other humans, if you use even a little AI, you risk the assumption that it is all AI and pointless to pay attention to</p>
</blockquote>

<p>In general, I agree with him, and react much the same. Once I hear LLM Voice, I flip the bozo bit and check out of the post.</p>

<p>But as I do this I have caveats. How accurate is my neural net at AI-detection, are humans taking on AI-ish elements in their normal communication? And more seriously there are some valid uses for AI. Jim Highsmith’s story of <a href="https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/60/9/677">his second brain helping him deal with Parkinson’s </a>is one. Another is folks who don’t know English well, using AI to translate. Am I being unfair when I flip the AI voice bit when I read/listen to them?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I was sad to hear that the <a href="https://anitab.org/press-release">Grace Hopper Celebration is canceled</a> this year. It’s an event run by Anitab.org to promote women and non-binary technologists in the software industry. Many of my colleagues have been encouraged and inspired by going to this event, which makes a refreshing change to what is often a male-dominated world. It seems the current crusade against diversity initiatives has had a big impact on funding. We are still a long way from where we should be on gender diversity in our profession, and losing an event like this only makes things more difficult.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve always found Cindy Sridharan’s writing to be worth reading, and she’s broken a quiet period with a monster of an article: <a href="https://copyconstruct.medium.com/how-to-effectively-debug-when-you-no-longer-understand-the-code-bd3dcb2c5f7b">How to effectively debug when you no longer understand the code</a>. Since Medium estimates it as a 64 minute read, I didn’t jump in lightly, but settled down on a flight from Amsterdam to Stuttgart. I’m glad I did, because there’s lot of good material in there, and I’d struggle to find anything to cut.</p>

<p>In the article she uses three recent bug hunts to surface principles for how to best use an AI tool for debugging. Much of this is governed by this wise observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Agents are not humans. They’re far more capable than humans when it comes to many things, and we should be leaning into these strengths, rather than trying to get agents to replicate human patterns of problem solving which might be suboptimal in agentic contexts.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>One conclusion from her experiences is that the code often isn’t the best thing for agents to look at</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>having an agent start an investigation by first reading the code is an anti-pattern. In fact, reading the code first to formulate hypotheses about a bug should always be the very last resort of the agentic investigation process, not the starting point. An agent should first collect all available evidence about the bug, and only then formulate the hypotheses from the code that might best explain what’s being observed.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>She distinguishes between a bottom tier of facts: raw data that agent can find about the behavior of the system - and an upper tier of hypotheses: inferences that the agents make based on that data.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Diagnosing a bug now becomes a problem of finding the hypothesis in the top tier that satisfies every fact from the bottom tier.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The agents need to be steered towards the hypotheses that are the likest cause of the bug and then subject these hypotheses to validation. She has a three-step process to do this. First she identifies the most important facts, the signals that might explain or identify the bug.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The factual tier has many data points. Not all of them are equally valuable. In fact, some of it might be active noise that doesn’t help an agent to be steered towards most promising hypothesis. The agent first needs to identify the data points in the factual tier that are actually related to the bug. Agents do this best when they are given a high-quality control</p>
</blockquote>

<p>A control is a behavior of the system that doesn’t exhibit the bug. This may be requests that trigger the bug compared to those that don’t, or different builds of the product, or the same build in different environments.</p>

<p>With signals highlighted, she then focuses on the hypotheses that are most promising, because they fit best with the signals. A hypothesis should satisfy all the facts, the most valuable signals are those that are only satisfied by a few hypotheses. A table of hypotheses and signals helps visualize the strength of the signals and the likelihood of the hypotheses.</p>

<p>The third step is then validating the promising hypotheses against every fact, treating each fact as constraint on the solution.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Across the three bugs chronicled in this post, my most valuable contributions weren’t knowledge of the code. They were picking a control, choosing a constraint, and rejecting a hypothesis that hadn’t been validated. The engineers who thrive in an agent-native world will be the ones who are good at designing such verification loops, whether or not they understand the code it verifies.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This directing has to remember the opening point that agents have different capabilities to humans.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A human wants to deal with less data. All abstractions we currently have (aggregation, visualizations etc) are designed to help humans deal with less data. An agent, however, needs more raw data; its strength lies in processing huge volumes of data at a rapid clip. Aggregation — the thing that makes a dashboard useful to a human — is often the thing that destroys the value of the signal for an agent</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Agents’ ability to consume lots of raw data also affects how we build systems. More logging gives agents more data to chew on.</p>

<p>And thus far, the human is still necessary to direct the agent</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>In each of the three bugs chronicled in this post, the agent was more than capable of getting to the root cause by itself, but it didn’t do so. It didn’t set up a control in the “latency bug” until I asked it to</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While it’s a long post, it’s engagingly written and full of useful details. I hope my little summary encourages you to give it the read it deserves.</p>

### [When Software Meets Hardware](https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>When software meets hardware, engineering principles remain largely the
    same, but the sensible defaults often change. <b class="author">Kuldeep Singh</b>
    describes our experience modernizing a large IoT fleet of battery-powered
    sensors. Our familiar principles remained important; what changed was how we
    applied them. These practices emerged through experiments, failures, and
    operational experience&#x2014;each challenge helping us replace a reasonable
    assumption with a more sensible default. </p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html">more…</a></p>

### [Build your slide template](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Building a slide template starts with three foundational choices &#x2014;
      colours, fonts and layouts. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> covers
      the basics of color theory and how to build a reusable palette, the
      essentials of typography, including serif, sans-serif, handwritten and
      monospace fonts, plus the finer points of kerning and leading, and why a
      near-blank layout might serve you better than the prescribed layouts that
      ship with your slideware.</p>

### [The Craft of Practitioner Voice](https://martinfowler.com/articles/practitioner-voice.html#TheCraftOfPractitionerVoice)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A couple of months ago <b class="author">Jim Highsmith</b> explained how
      practitioners writing for each other forms a distinct style of writing. He
      now adds a section to the article with more detail on the craft of how we
      write in this style. How the writer shows up in the prose, its four
      principles, the role of story, how to sensibly use AI, three levels of
      editing, and clarity of the audience.</p>

### [Are Harnesses Worth the Cost?](https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/donkey-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p><b class="author">Naresh Jain</b> compares coding agents building the same
      API service with and without a behavioral harness. A frontier model, GPT-6
      Sol, passed every external evaluation either way. But a cost-optimized
      model, GPT-6 Luna, succeeded in only 3 of 10 unharnessed runs and all 10
      with the harness. While adding the harness increased token usage and
      runtime for Luna, the harnessed Luna runs achieved the same 10-for-10
      reliability as GPT-6 Sol while costing 33% less per run ($0.273 vs
      $0.406). This experiment suggests a behavioral harness might be a
      practical way to get dependable results from smaller, cost-optimized
      models.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html">more…</a></p>

### [Fragments: October  4](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-04.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In response to my <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html">last fragments</a> (probably the bit about us worrying if LLMs have consciousness when we when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience) <a href="https://x.com/metalanguage_os/status/2104934457333707058">“Metalanguage” replied</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>we shipped the id and forgot the superego. classic software lifecycle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I don’t know what was on their mind, but their post immediately made me think of the classic 1956 movie Forbidden Planet. Plenty of sci-fi, and other literature, have explored humans creating technology with unintended behavior, going back at least to Mary Shelly. But that movie was particularly influential on sci-fi film-making and in the heart of its story is what happens when we nurture a thinking machine.</p>

<p>I use the term “nurture” here deliberately. We talk of building software, but building implies a degree of determinism. When we build a bridge, or a locomotive, we expect it to behave in a controlled and well-understood manner. That’s a difference in degree to how we cultivate plants in our garden, or nurture young children. One of the challenges of working with these systems is understanding what has changed in this shift from building a computational system to nurturing an inferential one, and how our processes need to change in response.</p>

<p>We get unintended behavior with deterministic building: some bridges have collapsed, and our  computational systems often have bugs. But one difference is that when we find a bug in a computational system we can usually fix it. Even if we can’t, we can usually disable a component so the bug won’t do further harm. With inferential LLMs however, there is no such simple fix or disablement, which may lead us to the fate of the Krell.</p>

<p>(If you haven’t seen Forbidden Planet, it’s well worth watching. Yes, it shows it was made in the 1950s - with special effects, music, acting, and attitudes of that decade. But the story is solid, and its key theme is very relevant to the future we build with generative AI. Just don’t read about it in advance, it’s better to be immersed in the story without spoilers - although my memory of that experience is understandably hazy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many people who follow me also know my friend Ola Bini, who was my colleague at Thoughtworks for many years, and was living in Ecuador working as an independent software security expert. Sadly his time in Ecuador was dogged by a <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2025/04/six-years-dangerous-misconceptions-targeting-ola-bini-and-digital-rights-ecuador">bogus prosecution</a> by the authorities there. But things seemed to have settled down, and although not allowed to leave Ecuador, Ola was able to get on with his life.</p>

<p>Sadly that’s no longer the case as he <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/ola-bini-ordered-leave-ecuador-under-obscure-accusations">was deported from Ecuador on Friday:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>According to information released by his lawyer, Bini was intercepted by a car with four people who identified themselves as immigration agents. He was then taken to an immigration office without further information or a formal order from a competent authority. There, officials told Bini that his visa had been revoked but didn’t show any supporting document.</p>

  <p>Bini’s defense filed a habeas corpus to safeguard his freedom and prevent his deportation. Yet, Ecuadorian authorities affirmed that the developer represents a threat or risk to public security and the state structure, and must leave the country. The ground for deportation is a secret report which allegedly asserts that Bini committed acts against the security of Ecuador. The defense could not access its contents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was really worried for a while, since it wasn’t clear where he was going to be deported to. But <a href="https://x.com/olabini/status/2106671967944511885">he tweeted from Sweden</a>, so I’m thankful for that. But this is only a partial relief. Ola has spent thirteen years in Ecuador and made it his home. To be thrown out of your home for scant reason is a heavy thing to bear, and the officials who did that have committed a serious offense.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DDD Europe have released the video of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fndU_B5rmPE">Gien Verschatse interviewing Eric Evans and myself</a> at the conference in June. We start by talking about how we bonded over conceptual modeling in the late 1990s. The conversation quickly moves to AI, we note that it’s impossible to predict how such a big change will work out. We do expect that it will cause us to think about our work in different ways, but the change may well be liberating, it’s reinvigorated Eric’s love of programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>people are probably going to feel very frustrated by [the new way of thinking about software]… but when you get through that, there is a kind of a wonderful feeling of my brain’s been loosened up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Our background in agile planning helps with the uncertainty, as we are used to taking small steps and being attentive to feedback. We mull on the interplay of writing and thinking, in terms of both prose and code, and how its very much an iterative process of exploration and refinement - the same is true when we chat with our LLMs.</p>

<p>And don’t miss Eric’s important final tip.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2106018287117345070">Paul Graham:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There were a lot of things that only worked because there’s a limit to the rate at which humans can operate. We’re about to find out what all of them are, as they break.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The speculation continues about whether or not reading code will play a part in a software developer’s future. <a href="https://ghuntley.com/readable/?ref=geoffrey-huntley-newsletter">Geoffrey Huntley says</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>People are still saying, very loudly, that code should be readable so that humans can understand it. I no longer think that’s the goal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Interestingly his example has the LLM explain a haskell function definition… by translating it to Python. Which, to me, suggests there is a role for code - just that LLM need not store code in the same form that it presents it to a reader. This is essentially the same idea as <a href="https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/ProjectionalEditing.html">projectional editing</a>, which posits that the editable representation of software need not be the same as its storage representation.</p>

<p>Sam Ruby touches on this as he <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/25/Pencils-Down-Notation-Up.html">muses on a Rails World keynote</a>. He quotes DHH saying:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Rust is a good prompt compilation target for the moment, but so is C++. And soon assembler. Then microcode. Myopic to think we’re going to stop the agentic drill bit until it reaches computing bedrock.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He responds with:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The post leaves one question unasked, though: what sits at the top of the drill? What do we keep, edit and trust as the source of truth?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He carries out exercise of looking at some Rails software. Represented in Ruby/Rails and its about 60,000 tokens. Compiling it into C it turns into 4,000,000 tokens. That increase in token size will hamper the LLM, that still has to fit it into its context window, and even if it were to fit, figure out where to focus its attention. Sam points out reasons why, even absent a human reading it, it makes sense to represent the program in a higher-level language.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>what Rails becomes when agents write the code: the most compact, precise and conventional specification of a web application, whatever it ends up compiled to.</p>

  <p>Let the drill go as deep as it can. Just keep the notation at the top.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This all reminds me of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html#CognitiveDebt">what Unmesh Joshi argued</a>: that code serves “two distinct
but intertwined purposes”: instructions to a machine, and a conceptual model of
the problem domain. After exploring how those change with LLMs he concludes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The role of coding is not disappearing. But it is changing.</p>

  <p>As LLMs make code generation cheaper, the mechanical act of writing instructions becomes less central. What becomes more important is making the conceptual model explicit, discovering the right vocabulary, and refining that vocabulary through iteration, domain expertise, and feedback. This is also why programming languages continue to matter deeply. We are not meant to be passive reviewers of generated code. The act of writing code is itself part of our thinking.</p>

  <p>Code is still instructions for a machine. But it is also a model of understanding. In the LLM era, that second role becomes even more important. The future of coding is not just writing more code faster. It is building better conceptual models, better vocabularies, and better foundations on top of which both humans and LLMs can work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In a later post, Sam <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/27/Partly-in-the-Right.html">pondered on how people are talking about the capabilities of agents</a> in a way that resembles the parable of the blind men and the elephant. We all only have only a partial view of this object and where it’s going. A theme for all of us:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The practical question isn’t whether agents are good. It’s this: for the task in front of you this week, where will the information come from, and what will check the result?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist’s pithy summation of <a href="https://www.economist.com/finance-and-economics/2026/09/22/you-cant-trade-your-way-through-the-ai-apocalypse">investors concerns about the dangers of AI companies’ products</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s hard to celebrate an initial public offering that leads to a terminal public offing.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The news about the latest model from Google <a href="https://x.com/aipulseda1ly/status/2105394296815779861">is interesting</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Gemini 4 Argon has an insanely low hallucination rate on Artificial Analysis. 15%.</p>

  <p>Grok 4.7 is at 29%. GPT-6 Astra 45%. Opus 5.5 59%. Fable 5.1 69%.</p>

  <p>The only models below it barely answer anything. None of them get more than 15% right.</p>

  <p>It gets fewer answers right than Opus 5.5 on max, 50% against 66%. But when it doesnt know, it says so instead of making something up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Being clearer about what it doesn’t know, at a cost of getting less answers right, is definitely a trade-off I prefer.</p>

---
